THE LEADER OF OPPOSITION (SHRI MALLIKARJUN KHARGE) : Hon. Chairman, 
Sir, on my own behalf and on behalf of all the Opposition Members, I extend heartiest 
felicitations on your assuming the august office of Chairman, Ra jya Sabha.  While I 
had already congratulated you on your electi on as the Vice-President of India, I again 
take this opportunity to reiterate the same on the floor of the House.    
 At the threshold, I wish to re-emphasize the critical importance of the office of 
the Chairman, Rajya Sabha, which the Co nstituent Assembly had intended.  Mr. 
Chairman, Sir, on your shoulders, rests the heavy responsibility of smooth conduct of 
the proceedings of the Council of States, which goes a long way in maintaining the 
dignity and pivotal relevance of the House. 
In this context, I consider it apt to quote the following words of Dr. Sarvepalli 
Radhakrishnan, which have b een spoken in a different way by our Deputy Chairman.  
8
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

This is a relevant portion. I am reading from Dr. Radhakrishnan’s speech.  The first 
Vice President and Chairman  of Rajya Sabha, during his acceptance speech on 16 th 
May, 1952, said, “I belong to no party. ” I am saying this because many people 
claimed that you belong to their party.  He said: “I belong to no party and, that 
means, I belong to every party in this House.   It shall be my endeavour to uphold the 
traditions, the highest traditions of the parliamentary democracy, and act towards 
every party with fairness and impartiality, wi th ill-will to none an d goodwill to all.  A 
democracy is likely to degenerate into a ty ranny if it does not allow the opposition 
groups to criticise fairly, freely and frankly the policies of the Government. ”  This is Dr. 
Sarvepalli Radhakrishnan’s speech.  So, I am quoting this, and this is the occasion to 
remind all of us in these circumstances.  Sir, valuable observations made by Dr.

South Indian region. At a time when concerns about federal imbalance and

Many have been quoted here.  Let me just quote Dr. B. R. Ambedkar because 
that is much more relevant as to what he expounded about the relevance and the 
cardinal role of the second Chamber, that is, Rajya Sabha.  He put across three vital 
responsibilities for Rajya Sabha.  First, it is a House for re vision and review.  That was 
the first vital responsibility.  Are we up to the mark or anywhe re near the mark?  
Second, it is to act as a check on hasty and ill-considered legislations.  I will give a 
glimpse of what is happening in this Ho use because you are new to this House.  
Between 2019 and 2024, 34 per cent of the Bills were passed with less than one hour 
of discussion in Rajya Sabha.  During the same period, around 60 per cent of the Bills 
were passed with less than two hours of disc ussion.  Is this what Dr. Ambedkar said 
about check on hasty and ill-considered legisl ations?  This question should ring in 
your ears.  The most important responsibili ty of this House, as expounded by Dr. 
Ambedkar, is to protect the States in a fede ral structure.  What is the status?  Our 
States have been bleeding. We are pleading …(Interruptions)… So, please think about 
the attitude of this House when federal issues come up.

SHRI PRAFUL PATEL (Maharashtra) :  Hon. Chairman, Sir, I join all distinguished 
colleagues and leaders in welcoming you to  this very august office, the second 
highest constitutional position of our country .  I have had the privilege of being your 
colleague in the Lok Sabha, when you were there, as well as having you as our hon. 
Governor in the State of Maharashtra. I can tell you with a lot of satisfaction and pride, 
and for the knowledge of Members, that I ha ve not seen a Governor who, in less than 
two months of assuming office, visited each and every district of Maharashtra.  And I 
am sure that would have applied to your ea rlier stints as Governor of Jharkhand, as 
Telangana Governor and as LG of Puducherry.  This itself demonstrates the keenness 
with which you would like to understand and see for yourself what the problems are, 
what the kind of developments are.  I am  sure, holding this august office, you will 
have the same vision for going yourself, not that you have not, in more detail, across 
the length and breadth of the country and un derstand the problems in detail yourself. 
I have been a Member of both the Houses ri ght from 1991 continuously.  I don’t want 
to call myself ‘senior’, but as somebody  who has experience in parliamentary 
democracy, I would only like to request you that the level of discourse, debate and 
engagement in the Rajya Sabha has been on a decline.  Various factors are there. If I 
say they are responsible, they will get very an gry or excited.  I don’t want to cast any 
aspersions on any side.                                                                                    
28
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

But somewhere a fine balance needs to be drawn.  If at all some Members 
have voiced the concern that Bills are being passed in less than two hours or more in 
din and shouting than through a discussion, who is responsible for that?  That should 
also be pondered upon by those who are making these kinds of statements.  I am 
sorry to say this, Sir, that today we are fe licitating you and the House is functioning 
very normally, but post these felicitations, we  are already aware what  is lying in store 
for us.  Not only for today, but for many da ys to come.  Again, I am not casting any 
aspersions. This is the way the whole par liamentary system has started functioning. 
You have been a parliamentarian and a Go vernor and you have held many public 
positions.   I would request you to try and find some fine balance in which all these 
concerns of both sides can be addressed.  At the same  time, Parliament has to 
function.  This is very much needed. People watch us.  People exp ect a lot of things 
from us. When we leave for Delhi during  the Parliament Session, everybody says, 
‘You are going for Parliament  Session!’ If somebody asks me whether I would raise 
these issues in Parliament, I am not in a position to say, ‘yes’.  I do not know whether 
I will even get the opportunity.  If the Hous e functions, only then something can be 
spoken here. I do not want to go into more  detail.  I welcome you. You are a person 
with the highest integrity and long public life.  Your demeanour and your personal 
rapport with people will go a long way. As I have seen in Maharashtra and in 
Parliament, you will definitely be able to enjoy the confidence of all sections of the 
House and perform your duties.  Not only duties towards the House, but towards our 
nation and nation building. I welcome you, Sir.  Thank you, Sir.

We all should be proud of India’s demo cratic power and celebrate her as the 
Mother of Democracy.  As already mentioned by many Members,  my humble journey 
from Tiruppur, the knitwear capital, to New Delhi, the national capital, shows the 
remarkable power of our democracy. 
Only in democracy, a person can rise from the humblest beginning to high 
positions in public life. This rise make s me more conscious of the duties and 
responsibilities of the Chair. This Chair had been adorned by the finest and most 
distinguished sons of this soil.  Their intellect, dignity, and commitment to 
parliamentary democracy have left behind an enduring legacy. And, I assure the 
House to continue their great legacy. We all had celebrated the 76th anniversary of the 
adoption of our Constitution as ‘Samvidhan Diwas’ in a grand manner.  It is essential 
for every citizen to live up to the constitution al values of justice, liberty, equality, and 
fraternity. These ideals ensu re that every citizen— rega rdless of the caste, creed, 
gender, language, region or religion— has a rightful place in our motherland.  My 
humble request to all the hon. Members of th is august House is that we all must act 
as exemplary leaders by upholding these constitutional values.  
 In the very first few days after my a ssumption of office, I met all the floor 
leaders of the Rajya Sabha. During the meet ing, I had stressed that the entire nation 
looks up to us for mature deliberations on  policies that will shap e our future.  Being 
the Members of the House of Elders, our Dharma is to work with commitment to fulfill 
the people’s rightful aspirations. Rajya Sa bha has been the cradle of many profound 
debates and discussions, whic h have helped in shaping the destiny of our nation. 
Each time we enter this temple of democracy, we should pledge to ourselves to follow 
the basic principles of a parliamentary democracy, which are: Dialogue, Deliberation, 
Debate, and Discussion. The Rajya Sabha, as the Council of States, reflects the 
federal character of our Republic. This House is also blessed with the knowledge and 
[ 01 December, 2025 ]
49

expertise of Nominated Members.  Together, we showcase the collective wisdom and 
diversity of our nation. 
  Our country is marching forward as a leader at the forefront of development in 
every sphere.  Citizens look up to the Par liament as the highest forum of wisdom and 
collective judgment to guide the nation. On this note,  I  am  reminded  of  the   words   
of    the   great Tamil saintly poet, Thiruvalluvar: &“Deliver your speech, after ensuring 
that no counter speech can  defeat  your own. ”  Thiruvalluvar says , “Speak only 
words that are useful and meaningful; Avoid words that are not in the interest of the 
society”.  This simple yet profound teaching holds immense relevance for all of us 
who shoulder the responsibility of nation building. We do not have any right to hurt the 
feelings of others. We must have toleranc e for different views of others.  The words 
spoken within this House shape policies, guide society, and influence the well-being 
of crores of our Bharat Vasis.  
I had been the Member of the 12
th and 13 th Lok Sabha. So, I can easily 
understand the challenges of an hon. Member of the House.  As Parliamentarians, we 
have a wide range of issues to cover.   As the House, we have a vast amount of 
business to transact.  Time will be a challenging factor for both of us--you and the 
Chair.  Every moment we discharge our responsibility in the House will move the 
nation in the positive direction.  
 Parliamentary practice and procedures such as Question Hour, Zero Hour and 
Special Mentions provide ample opportunities to every individual Member to raise the 
pressing issues and concerns of the citizens in the House. I request and urge upon all 
of you to make effective use of these devices. 
Every parliamentary device we use with  sincerity becomes a step towards 
justice, equality, and human dignity. Let us commit ourselves to ensuring that our 
actions inside this House reflect the aspira tions of every farmer, every worker, every 
street vendor, every woman and youth and th e poorest of the poor who look at the 
Parliament with lot of hopes.  We need to fulfill our constitutional commitment towards 
social justice and economic empowerment of Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, 
Backward Classes and the other weaker sections of the society.

OBITUARY REFERENCES 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Hon. Members, it is with profound  sorrow that I refer to the passing 
away of Shrimati Chandra Kala Pandey, Prof. Vijay Kumar Malhotra and Shri Ashk Ali 
Tak, former Members of this House. 
Shrimati Chandra Kala Pandey  passed away on the 17
th of September, 2025, 
at the age of 83 years. She served as a Member of Rajya Sabh a representing the 
State of West Bengal for two consecutive terms, from August, 1993 to August, 1999 
and August, 1999 to August, 2005. 
Born on 8 th June, 1942 at Azamgarh in Utta r Pradesh, Shrimati Pandey was a 
distinguished academician, an accomplished Hindi scholar, and a dedicated public 
representative. She served as a Professor and Head of the Department of Hindi at the 
[ 01 December, 2025 ]
51

University of Calcutta, where she guided generations of students and earned deep 
respect for her scholarship, simplicity, and humane approach. 
  A noted poet, she authored two acclaimed poetry collections titled ‘Aakash 
Kahan Hai’ and ‘Utsav Nahin Hain Mere Shabda’. Her collection of poems was also 
released as an album ‘ Sagar Pakhi ’ in 2001. She contributed extensively to Hindi 
literary discourse and served as co-e ditor of the well-known magazine ‘ Samayik 
Chintan’ for many years.  
As a parliamentarian, Shrimati Pandey  brought to the proceedings of this 
House the perspective of an educationist, a literary figure, and a public intellectual 
deeply invested in social issues.   
In the passing away of Shrimati Chan dra Kala Pandey, the country has lost a 
respected academic, a gifted poet, and a dedicated parliamentarian. 
Prof. Vijay Kumar Malhotra  passed away on the 30th of September, 2025, at 
the age of 93 years.  He served as a Memb er of the Rajya Sabha representing the 
National Capital Territory of Delhi, from January, 1994 to October, 1999.   
Born on 3rd December, 1931, at Lahore in undivided India, Prof. Malhotra, a 
distinguished public leader, devoted more than six decades of his life to public 
service, emerging as one of the foremost political figures in the National Capital.   
Prof. Malhotra began his career as a Lect urer at Delhi Univer sity and was later 
elected as the Chief Executive Councillor of Delhi in 1967.  He served multiple terms in 
public office, including four terms as a Me mber of the Lok Sabha from Delhi and two 
terms as a Member of the Delhi Legislativ e Assembly.  Prof. Ma lhotra was widely 
respected for his simplicity, integrity, orga nisational skills, and deep understanding of 
constitutional and public issues.  He also ma de significant contributions in the field of 
sports administration, for which he was fondly remembered by colleagues across 
political lines.   
In the passing away of Prof. Vijay Ku mar Malhotra, the nation has lost a 
veteran statesman, an experienced parliament arian, and a distinguished public figure 
who played an influential role in shaping contemporary political life in Delhi. 
  Shri Ashk Ali Tak  passed away on the 5th of October, 2025 at the age of 67 
years.  He served as a Memb er of Rajya Sabha representing the State of Rajasthan, 
from July, 2010 to July, 2016.   
52
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Born on 7th July, 1958, in Hanumangarh District of Rajasthan, Shri Ashk Ali Tak 
was a distinguished public representative  whose political journey spanned several 
decades.  He served as a Member of Raja sthan State Legislative Assembly for two 
terms and as a Minister in the Government of Rajasthan holding several portfolios.  He 
also served as the Chairman of the Raja sthan State Minorities Commission.  Known 
for his deep grassroots connect, Shri Tak was widely admired for his approachability 
and organisational capability.  Throughout his political career, he worked actively for 
the welfare of minorities, youth and socially disadvantaged communities.   
In the passing away of Shri Ashk A li Tak, the country has lost a capable 
administrator, a dedicated social and political worker and an able parliamentarian. 
  We deeply mourn the passing away of  Shrimati Chandra Kala Pandey, Prof. 
Vijay Kumar Malhotra and Shri Ashk Ali Tak.

respect to the memory of the departed.

FELICITATIONS BY THE CHAIR 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Hon. Members, with immense pride and joy, I extend my heartiest 
congratulations to our women sportspersons who have brought laurels to the nation 
by their extraordinary achievements. 
Hon. Members, as you are aware, our nation has created history by winning 
the ICC Women's ODI Cricket World Cup for the first time, defeating South Africa by 
52 runs in the final match held at Navi Mumbai on 2nd November 2025. Our women's 
cricket team, led by Miss Harmanpreet Ka ur, delivered a magnificent all-round 
performance to achieve this landmark victory. 
Hon. Members, our nation also achie ved another proud milestone when our 
blind women's cricket team won the in augural Blind Women's T20 World Cup, 
defeating Nepal by seven wickets in the fina l match held in Colombo, Sri Lanka, on 
23rd November 2025. The team, led by Miss Deepika TC, put up an inspiring 
performance. In yet another remarkable feat, the Indian Women’s Kabaddi  Team led 
by Ms. Ritu Negi won the Women’s Kaba ddi World Cup for the second successive 
time by defeating Chinese Taipei in the fina l match held at Dhaka, Bangladesh on the 
24th of November, 2025.  
These back-to-back global victories by our women sportspersons mark a 
golden moment in Indian sports. These tr iumphs not only reflect the courage, 
consistency and discipline of our women sp ortspersons but are also testaments to 
the long, arduous yet remarkable and fulf illing journey that our women sportspersons 
have traversed for these momentous occas ions. These achievements underscore the 
growing excellence of Indian women in diverse sporting arenas. 
  On my own behalf and on behalf of the whole House, I extend our warmest 
congratulations to all the three teams, th eir coaches and the support staff. Their 
achievements have elevated India’s honour and inspired millions across the nation.

Concern over Banana crisis in Rayalaseema region of Andhra Pradesh 
SHRI SUBHAS CHANDRA BOSE PILLI (Andhra Pradesh) :   Sir, banana is the crucial 
crop for small and marginal farmers in Rayala seema Region of Andhra Pradesh.  A    
few   years   ago,   farmers   in    the drought-prone Rayalaseema adopted Tissue 
Culture Banana (Variety “G9/Cavendish), which reportedly gave high yields (60-70 
tonnes per acre versus older 20 tonnes per acre only).  This success had encouraged 
many farmers across the drought-hit zones,  like Kadapa, Kurnool and Anantapur, to 
shift to balance cultivation.  In one of the main Banana-growing zones, Pulivendula 
(Kadapa), banana recently fetched Rs.22,000 per tonne, but within a month, the 
price dropped to Rs.8,000 per tonne, that is, 60 per cent collapse.  Many farmers say 
that they got good yield but could not sell due to some ripe branches rotted on the 
plant or were thrown away.  For 2025 Khar if Season, due to drought and erratic 
rainfall, cultivation dropped significantly and in some districts, like YSR Kadapa 
District, only 19-35 per cent growing was done as compared to normal.  Because of a 
sharp drop in Banana prices, the hon. Chie f Minister of Andhra Pradesh has ordered 
that bananas grown in Rayalaseema region be quickly transported to major demand 
centres like Mumbai and Kolkata by using rail cargo to prevent farmer losses.  That is 
the only greatest help to farmers of Raya laseema region by our State Government.  
That is the pity.  
Our Party President and form er Chief Minister of Andhra Pradesh, Shri Y.S. 
Jagan Mohan Reddy garu, has visited the banana fields of Pulivendula Region and 
64
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

intimated me to place before the Central Government, through you, Sir, for immediate 
relief to the farmers of Rayalaseema Region.  I request the Agriculture Ministry to take 
necessary steps to help the distressed farm ers and keep in mind that Tissue Culture 
methods are not enough to guarantee stable income if market, prices or fate turns 
bad.

raised by Shri Subhas Chandra Bose Pilli.

Concern over air pollution in Delhi-NCR 
DR. M. THAMBIDURAI (Tamil Nadu):  Sir, I want to bring to the notice of the House 
the very serious pollution situation in Delhi-NCR.  Every year, we sa y that things will 
be improved, but every winter, the same crisis returns, and this year is no different.  
Air has become so polluted that people are genuinely scared to step out.  Children 
are coughing, senior citizens are struggling to breathe and many people are avoiding 
outdoor work because of the air simply not being safe.  Schools had to be shut down 
again.  Hospitals are reporting rise in cases  of asthma and respiratory illness.  People 
are living with masks even within the houses .   T h i s  i s  n o t  n o r m a l  a n y m o r e .   I t  h a s  
become unbearable.   
What is most disappointing is that de spite so many meetings, committees and 
court directions, the ground situation has hardly improved.  Stubble burning 
continues, dust from construction is ever ywhere, waste burning is happening openly 
and the traffic pollution remains unchecked.  This is also surprising that vehicles, that 
had already completed their permitted life, have been allowed to run for one more 
year, which will add to the problems instead of solution. 
So, through this Zero Hour submission, I sincerely urge up on the Government 
to treat this as an emergency situation.  We need strong and co-ordinated action, not 
just temporary steps, for the people of th e Delhi-NCR.  I am also facing similar 
problem in my throat and that is why, I am raising this issue here.

Haque; not present.  Now, Shri Sujeet  Kumar-- Demand to include Sarhul in 
UNESCO’s Intangible Cultural Heritage list. 
Demand to include Sarhul in UNESCO’s Intangible Cultural Heritage list 
SHRI SUJEET KUMAR (Odisha): Sir, I draw your attention to Sarhul, a vibrant festival 
celebrated across Jharkhand and echoed across Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Bengal, 
Assam and the Northeastern hills. Sarhul, meaning worship of the Sal flower, marks 
the arrival of spring and the tribal New Year . It is more than a celebration; it is an 
[ 01 December, 2025 ]
73

expression of ecological gratitude, where communities offer prayers to nature, spirits, 
and ancestors. At the heart of this tradition stands the Sal tree, a symbol of continuity 
and reverence.  
 The Sal tree finds mention in our epics and faiths. Lord Ram is said to have 
used a Sal tree to string his bow. Ga utama Buddha was born under the one and 
attained Mahaparinirvana between the two.  Lord Mahavira is believed to have found 
enlightenment under its shade. Tribal communities have long used its leaves for 
making plates, its resin for traditional medicine . It is not just a tree; it is our history 
with roots. What makes Sarhul extraordinary is its oral continuity. There are no written 
texts, no fixed rituals. Yet it has survi ved for centuries, carried forward through 
songs, storytelling, and shared memory.  
India currently has 14 elements on UNESCO’s Intangible Cultural Heritage list. I 
urge the Government, through you, to begin the process of nominating Sarhul.  It is 
not just a festival, it is a way of life, a reminder of how India has long known to live 
with the forest, not beyond it. I, therefor e, request the Government to take necessary 
steps for including Sarhul in UNESCO’s Intangible Cultural Heritage list. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  The hon. Member, Shrimati Su mitra Balmik, (Madhya Pradesh), 
associated herself with the Special Mention made by the hon. Member, Shri Sujeet 
Kumar.

Shri Brij Lal ; concern over negati

Concern over negative effects of processed and packaged foods on 
 health of children 
SHRI BRIJ LAL (Uttar Pradesh) : Sir, the processed and packaged foods like ready 
meals, baked goods and processed meats ne gatively impact health by being in 
unhealthy fats, salts and sugar, while being low in essential nutri ents like fibre and 
vitamins.  Indian metro society is also adapting processed and packaged foods due 
to nuclear family, boarding living, absence of joint family, metro lifestyle, work in 
shifts, educational and job opportunities, etc.   This can lead to increased risks of 
obesity, heart disease, type 2 diabetes and other chronic conditions.  Chemically 
processed foods also called as  ultra processed foods, tend t o  b e  h i g h  i n  s u g a r ,  
artificial ingredients, refined carbohydra tes and trans fats.  Ultra processed food 
intake has increased dramatically worldw ide and now accounts for 25 to 60 per cent 
of the daily calorie.  This affe cts health in many ways like weight and obesity, chronic 
diseases, nutritional deficiencies, increa sed inflammation leading to digestive 
problems to autoimmune disorders, gut he alth disruption.  Some processed foods 
are engineered to be highly palatable an d can trigger cravings leading to over 
consumption.   These are all potential for lo ng term harm.  We can make it healthier 
by replacing our focus on whole foods, by  checking the label fr om the long list of 
ingredients and limiting sugar, salt and unh ealthy fats in processed snacks, sauces 
and pre-packaged meals.  I urge upon the Government to look into it.

‘Concern over decline of single-screen theatres in India.’ 
Concern over decline of Single-Screen theatres in India. 
SHRI S NIRANJAN REDDY (Andhra Pradesh): Sir, my subject is concern over decline 
of Single-Screen theatres in India.  India’s cinema culture is facing an unprecedented 
crisis.  One research shows that India now has fewer than 6,000 single-screen 
theatres, as compared to 25,000 theatres in the 1990s. In May 2024, this crisis 
worsened as 450 single screens in Telang ana were shut down temporarily due to 
extremely low footfalls. There are severa l reasons for the collapse – multiplex 
expansion, high production and distributi on costs, rising ticket prices, and the 
pandemic-driven shift to OTT (over-the-top) platforms, which have steadily eroded 
theatre audiences. 
For single screens in smaller towns, high operational expenses and limited 
access to credit have pushed many to perm anent closure. An af fordability crisis is 
also looming over single-screen t
heatres.  Th is is driven by surge-priced tickets, 18 
per cent GST, platform fees, and exorbitant F&B rates. 
[ 01 December, 2025 ]
77

While the industry cites a national averag e ticket price of Rs. 130, the urban 
reality is starkly different. PVR INOX's average ticket price reached  Rs. 254 in quarter 
one of the Financial year 2026, alongside record food spending. Yet, even multiplexes 
are struggling, posting a Rs. 114.3 crore loss in the Financial Year 2024, revealing a 
model that neither sustains audiences nor businesses. 
This moment calls for urgent policy ac tion: GST relief for single screens, 
concessional loans, renovation support, nati onal regulation of concession pricing, 
and swift single-window theatre clearances. Without timely intervention, India loses a 
cultural institution that has shaped generations. I urge the Government to look into it.
